People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling like I’m always moving from one thing to the next. I’ve usually managed okay, or at least kept it from being too noticeable, but with the new setup at work it’s been harder to sort of… hide it or work around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it obvious. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: Sitting still through a training video or a slow appointment makes me feel kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up, stretch, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet stuff that’s supposed to relax me, like reading at home or sitting through a movie with my wife, doesn’t last long before I’m shifting around, commenting, or getting up for something.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I feel like I’m always moving from one thing to the next, like I can’t just idle, and the new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn in a team huddle or in the help queue makes me antsy, and I catch myself checking the screen over and over or wanting to jump in before I’m called on.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I don’t mean to be rude, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll jump in with an answer before they’ve finished or before anyone asked me.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Looking back, this was around long before this job change; in grade school my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, talked out of turn, and had to be reminded to stay with my work.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has started showing up in my reviews, with supervisors pointing out unfinished tickets and mistakes that slow the team down even though I’m trying hard and generally get along well with customers.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about 1 year and 10 months, since my job shifted into the new hybrid chat and CRM role.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse when you're alone, like buying things or making decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big issue for me. I mean, I probably do little things sometimes without thinking it all the way through, like anyone, but I can’t think of any major examples with buying things or big decisions. If you mean something specific, I can try to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: As a kid… I think I was probably a little more restless than some, but I don’t know if it was extreme. Even now, sitting through something slow, like a training video or a long appointment, I get this kind of boxed-in feeling, like I need to stand up, stretch, or at least keep my hands busy. So I’m guessing that was there when I was younger too, just maybe not something people made a big deal about.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like making sudden decisions, without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Not really in any big way, I don’t think. I mean, I’m sure I make quick calls sometimes, but I usually try to think things through, especially if it’s something important. I wouldn’t want to say no if there’s something I’m missing, but nothing really stands out to me as a frequent problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of time or forget about everything else around you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’ve usually managed okay, or at least kept it from being too noticeable, but with the new setup at work it’s been harder to sort of… hide it or work around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it obvious. It’s not like I’m jumping out of my chair or anything. But even when I’m trying to sit there and look calm, my foot’s usually bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen in a meeting without really realizing I’m doing it. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

A little, yeah. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I do try hard and I think I get along with customers pretty well. It’s not like I’m jumping out of my chair or anything. But even when I’m trying to sit there and look calm, my foot’s usually bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen in a meeting without really realizing I’m doing it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but it does happen. Like I’ll think I’ve finished a customer ticket and then later I realize I typed in the wrong account number, or I skipped one of the required boxes. Sometimes it’s just one little instruction I missed, and then the whole thing comes back to me to fix. It’s not constant, but enough that I feel bad about it, you know?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with attention?\n\nPatient: I guess… looking back, it wasn’t just with this job change. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but even in grade school my teachers would write things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn. And I remember being reminded to stay with my work. So… it’s probably been there a long time, I just kind of worked around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before questions have been finished?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. I try not to, because I don’t want to come across like I think I know better. But if I hear coworkers talking through a customer problem, I might jump in with the answer before they’ve really finished explaining it, or before anyone actually asked me. I usually mean it as being helpful, not rude… but I can see how it might feel like interrupting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself speaking out of turn in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do sometimes. I try to be respectful, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer issue, I might jump in with an answer before they’re done explaining it, or before anyone really asked me. I don’t mean it in a rude way… I’m usually just trying to help.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse when you're alone, like buying things or making decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big issue for me. I mean, I probably do little things sometimes without thinking it all the way through, like anyone, but I can’t think of any major examples with buying things or big decisions. If you mean something specific, I can try to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: As a kid… I think I was probably a little more restless than some, but I don’t know if it was extreme. Even now, sitting through something slow, like a training video or a long appointment, I get this kind of boxed-in feeling, like I need to stand up, stretch, or at least keep my hands busy. So I’m guessing that was there when I was younger too, just maybe not something people made a big deal about.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like making sudden decisions, without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Not really in any big way, I don’t think. I mean, I’m sure I make quick calls sometimes, but I usually try to think things through, especially if it’s something important. I wouldn’t want to say no if there’s something I’m missing, but nothing really stands out to me as a frequent problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of time or forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it intense, exactly. I mean, if I’m doing something I’m interested in, I can get into it and maybe lose track of time a little, but I think that’s pretty normal, right? I haven’t really noticed it being a major problem where I forget everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Were you often running, climbing, or playing physical games more than other kids when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember being the kid who was always climbing on things or running nonstop, no. I mean, maybe I was a bit restless, but not, you know, wildly more than everyone else. The part I notice more now is when I have to sit still through something slow, like a training video or an appointment, I start feeling kind of boxed in and want to stand up or stretch or keep my hands busy. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems or distress in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I do try hard and I think I get along with customers pretty well. But at work it has started coming up in my reviews—my supervisors have mentioned unfinished tickets and some mistakes that slow the team down. That’s been kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but looking back it wasn’t just something from this job. In grade school, I remember teachers writing things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn. And I had to be reminded to stay with my work. I guess at the time it was just seen as, you know, “he could do better if he applied himself.” Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

If you mean something specific, I can try to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: As a kid… I think I was probably a little more restless than some, but I don’t know if it was extreme. I haven’t really noticed it being a major problem where I forget everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Were you often running, climbing, or playing physical games more than other kids when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember being the kid who was always climbing on things or running nonstop, no. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems or distress in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I do try hard and I think I get along with customers pretty well. But at work it has started coming up in my reviews—my supervisors have mentioned unfinished tickets and some mistakes that slow the team down. That’s been kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but looking back it wasn’t just something from this job. I guess at the time it was just seen as, you know, “he could do better if he applied himself.” Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention being present both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it out, honestly. If you mean something specific, I can try to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: As a kid… I think I was probably a little more restless than some, but I don’t know if it was extreme. Even now, sitting through something slow, like a training video or a long appointment, I get this kind of boxed-in feeling, like I need to stand up, stretch, or at least keep my hands busy. So I’m guessing that was there when I was younger too, just maybe not something people made a big deal about.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like making sudden decisions, without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Not really in any big way, I don’t think. I mean, I’m sure I make quick calls sometimes, but I usually try to think things through, especially if it’s something important. I wouldn’t want to say no if there’s something I’m missing, but nothing really stands out to me as a frequent problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of time or forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it intense, exactly. I mean, if I’m doing something I’m interested in, I can get into it and maybe lose track of time a little, but I think that’s pretty normal, right? I haven’t really noticed it being a major problem where I forget everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Were you often running, climbing, or playing physical games more than other kids when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember being the kid who was always climbing on things or running nonstop, no. I mean, maybe I was a bit restless, but not, you know, wildly more than everyone else. The part I notice more now is when I have to sit still through something slow, like a training video or an appointment, I start feeling kind of boxed in and want to stand up or stretch or keep my hands busy. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems or distress in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I do try hard and I think I get along with customers pretty well. But at work it has started coming up in my reviews—my supervisors have mentioned unfinished tickets and some mistakes that slow the team down. That’s been kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be careless.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but looking back it wasn’t just something from this job. In grade school, I remember teachers writing things like I was bright, but careless, or that I talked out of turn. And I had to be reminded to stay with my work. I guess at the time it was just seen as, you know, “he could do better if he applied himself.” Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention being present both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it out, honestly. At work it’s easier to notice because there are, you know, tasks and supervisors seeing it. At home… I guess I don’t pay as much attention to it, or it doesn’t feel as obvious. I might get a little antsy if I’m sitting too long, but I wouldn’t want to say it’s a big problem there unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, everybody has little misunderstandings sometimes, but I can’t really point to relationships being seriously damaged because of it. At least not that I’ve noticed. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with attention in multiple settings, such as both at work and in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed it in social situations, to be honest. Work, yes, that’s where it stands out more because I’m expected to stay on one thing and there are more consequences. Socially… I might get a little restless if something goes on a long time, but I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly seen it as a problem there. Is that the kind of setting you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been more noticeable for… maybe just under two years now. Around a year and ten months, I guess, since my job changed into that hybrid chat and CRM role. Before that I could manage okay, or at least I thought I could, but since then it’s been harder to keep it under control. Does that answer what you mean by consistently?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with restlessness, attention, or impulsivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I guess there were signs even when I was a kid. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, because I did okay in school, but I remember teachers saying I was bright but careless. They’d write things like I talked out of turn or needed reminders to stay with my work.\n\nSo, yes, I think before 12 there were some of those attention and restlessness-type issues. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with restlessness and attention for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely more than six months. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been noticeably harder to manage for about a year and ten months now, since my job shifted into that hybrid chat and CRM role. That’s when I really started feeling like it was harder to keep on top of.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with restlessness and attention for at least 6 months prior to your job change?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, honestly. Before the job change, I had some habits or little issues here and there, but I don’t know that I’d call them “significant” for six months or more. It became much more obvious after the role changed. Is that the distinction you’re trying to get at?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
